CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION This Eighth Semiannual Report of the Small Business 

Administration summarizes the activities of the Agency in the three areas assigned to it by Congress: 1. 2. 3. 

Helping small business gain access to adequate capital and credit;

Helping small business obtain a fair share of Government procurement; and

Helping small business obtain competent management, technical, and production counsel.

In addition, this report reviews various aspects of the changing economic position of small business in our economy. 

The various Small Business Administration programs registered marked progress during this period. Highlighting the progress in the financial assistance area, 1,936 business loans for $89, 035, 954 were approved between January 1 and June 30, 1957, compared with $70, 058, 890 during the previous 6-months period. For fiscal year 1957, business and disaster loan approvals totaled 5, 133 for $172, 087,000 compared with 5, 224 approvals for $126, 379, 000 during fiscal year 1956. During fiscal 1957, business loan applications continued the upward trend which the Agency has experienced each year since the beginning of the loan program on September 29, 1953. The 

Agency received in fiscal 1957 a total of 6, 864 applications, compared with 4, 081 in 1956, 3, 318 in 1955 and 2, 289 in 1954. The monthly average of 572 applications in 1957 represents an increase of 68.2 per cent over the monthly average of 340 in 1956, which, in turn, reflected a 23. 2 per cent increase over the 1955 monthly 1

average of 276. During the fiscal year 1957, the Small Business 

Administration approved 58.7 per cent of the business loan applications acted on (approved and declined), a substantial increase over the 45.4 per cent rate on a cumulative basis through June 30, 

Highlighting the progress in the area of Government procurement, set-aside actions earmarking Government purchases for exclusive award to small business firms increased sharply. The number of Government procurements set aside for small business through joint action by the Small Business Administration and Government purchasing agencies increased 80.9 per cent in this 6-months period compared to the last half of fiscal year 1956. The number of contract awards resulting from these set asides increased by 84. 2 per cent. On a dollar value basis procurements set aside for small business increased 30.9 per cent and contract awards resulting from the set asides, 79 per cent. Other phases of the Agency's Procurement Assistance Program also showed significant increases in activity. For example, contacts with prime contractors to develop subcontracting opportunities for small firms increased 18. 8 per cent in this period compared with the last half of fiscal year 1956, and the 

Agency made 65.9 per cent more referrals of subcontracting opportunities to small firms. 

This report discusses in detail the statutory responsibilities assigned to the Small Business Administration by the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended. Many of the activities of this Agency during the past year have been conducted in cooperation with other Government agencies with the Small Business Administration serving as a spokesman and advocate of small business. Effectiveness of this Agency has been expanded through use of the personnel of other Government agencies to make studies pertaining to the problems of small business. 

The Agency worked in collaboration with the Securities and Exchange Commission on a proposed revision of Regulation A. 

In addition, the Small Business Administration has set up a continuing liaison with the Commission to provide a constant review of small business equity financing problems. Officials of the Small 

Business Administration testified before the Senate Committee on Banking and Currency on a bill to increase from $300,000 to $500,000 the maximum amount of an issue of corporate securities which the Securities and Exchange Commission may exempt from registration.

Agency officials conferred with officials of the Civil Aeronautics Board about methods of improving the financial position of small feeder airlines by loans or reassignment of routes. They conferred also with representatives of the Civil 

Aeronautics Administration about loans to private airports for hangar facilities.

Representatives of this Agency conferred with officials of the Interstate Commerce Commission regarding problems of small firms in the trucking industry, and established liaison with the Interstate Commerce Commission to obtain from it needed credit information.

Officials of the Agency also have consulted with officials of the Federal Communications Commission to develop information concerning the problems of the smaller ultra-high frequency stations. They studied the impact of the proposed subscription television on the financial position of UHF stations, which are predominantly small, and the further effect the authorization of subscription television would have on increased advertising opportunities for small business. 

Close liaison has been established with the Department of Agriculture on the drought programs of both the Small Business Administration and the Department of Agriculture. Representatives of the Small Business Administration and the Department of Agriculture have conferred jointly with financial institutions in an effort to meet the credit needs of both business men and farmers in the drought areas. The Administrator was asked by the Department of Agriculture to serve as a member of the Committee for Rural Development Program, along with the Under Secretaries of several Government Departments. This committee develops policies to improve the economic position of low-income areas. 

A substantial percentage of Small Business Administration loans have been made to small business concerns which are closely related to agriculture. Officials of this Agency conferred with officials of the Agriculture Department on problems confronting the poultry industry and the relationship of our loan program to the poultry lending programs of other agencies. The Agency has been working closely with the Housing and Home Finance Agency in connection with its Community

Facilities Disposal Program. This program is designed to dispose of Government-owned property located at Oak Ridge, Tennessee, and Richland, Washington (pursuant to the Atomic Energy Act of 1955). Financial specialists of this Agency process all applications for commercial financing for prospective buyers of these properties. 

Where applicants are not eligible for assistance from this Agency, Housing and Home Finance Agency officials determine whether to furnish financial assistance. 

In co-operation with the Departments of Commerce and Justice, the Export-Import Bank, the Federal Trade Commission, the International Cooperation Administration, the Internal Revenue 

Service and faculty members of the Harvard University Graduate 

School of Business Administration, the Small Business Adminis tration has developed information of value to small business concerns desiring to enter the international trade field. The Agency has issued a publication entitled "Pointers on International Trade, which has received favorable comment and wide acceptance. 

In the Disaster Loan Program, the Small Business Ad ministration has worked in close co-operation with the Federal 

Civil Defense Administration, the Housing and Home Finance 

Agency and the American Red Cross in assuring assistance to the victims of disasters. 11 The Agency has co-operated with the Fish and Wildlife Service of the Department of the Interior in establishing and ad ministering its Fisheries Loan Program. The Small Business 

Administration is developing the necessary credit information for such loans, and the loans are being closed and administered through the Agency's regional offices. 

The Small Business Administration has established a continuing liaison with the Antitrust Division of the Department of Justice whereby the Agency has obtained lists of inventions and patents made available for licensing to the public through judgments in antitrust cases, and has published abstracts of these patents in the monthly Products List Circular. 

The Agency is collaborating with the Business and 

Defense Services Administration of the Department of Commerce to assist small business concerns in obtaining access to scarce materials.

The Small Business Administration has co-operated actively with other agencies in implementing recommendations of the President's Cabinet Committee on Small Business. On 

July 15, 1957, the President sent a letter to the Chairman of the Committee on Ways and Means of the House of Representatives, suggesting that the committee consider favorably the following tax recommendations of the President's Cabinet Committee: 

1. 2. 3. 

That businesses be given the right to utilize, for purchases of used property not exceeding $50, 000 in any one year, the formulas of accelerated depreciation that were made available to purchasers of new property by the Internal Revenue 

Code of 1954. 

That corporations with, say, 10 or fewer stockholders be given the option of being taxed as if they were partnerships. 

That the taxpayer be given the option of paying the estate tax over a period of up to 10 years in cases where the estate consists largely of investments in closely held business concerns. In addition, the President recommended one additional change in the law to permit an original investor in small business the right to deduct from his income, up to a maximum amount prescribed by Congress, a loss, if any, realized on a stock investment in such business. At the present time, the deduction of such losses from income is subject to the general limitation on net capital losses of $1,000. This recommendation is of great significance by reason of the continuing difficulties encountered by small firms in obtaining equity capital. From the investigation of this subject by the Small Business Administration, it is concluded that the proposed change would provide a great stimulus to investment of equity capital in eligible small firms.

Each of the tax recommendations could be helpful in the financing, operation or continued independent existence of small businesses. 

In regard to the proposal of the Cabinet Committee that taxes imposed on business corporations be modified by reducing

the tax rate from 30 per cent to 20 per cent on incomes up to $25,000, the President stated that adoption of this recommendation would be ill-advised because of the substantial revenue loss that it would entail. Also, in the absence of a general tax reduction, which the budgetary situation does not permit at this time, the President stated that a tax reduction of this character would discriminate against the many small businesses which are conducted in the form of partnerships or individual proprietorships. 

In his letter, the President gave a current status report on the 10 additional recommendations made by his Cabinet Committee on Small Business. Some of these recommendations have been carried out by the Executive Branch, while others must await Congressional action before the Executive Branch can act upon them. The President's review follows: "In its Recommendation No. 5, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That the President arrange for a comprehensive review of procurement policies and procedures of all departments and agencies, including the legislation pertaining thereto, with a view to facilitating and extending the participation of small businesses in work on Government contracts.' "On September 26, 1956, I directed the Administrator of the General Services Administration to plan and conduct such a review, in cooperation with other major procurement agencies. 

The First Summary Report of the Task Force set up by the 

Administrator of the General Services Administration under this 

Directive was issued on March 1, 1957. Several important improvements in procurement procedures have already been accomplished as a result of the Task Force efforts, and a comprehensive proposal for amendments to the procurement laws has been developed by the Task Force and is currently being reviewed by the cognizant executive agencies. The purpose of the amendments being reviewed would be to bring about greater uniformity and simplification of Government procurement procedures, and to improve the opportunities of small businesses to participate in Government work. "In its Recommendation No. 6, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That the President direct departments and agencies engaged in extensive procurement to adopt procedures which would insure that a need for advance or progress payments by a bidder will not be treated as a handicap in awarding a contract,

and which would facilitate and accelerate the making of such progress payments as may be requested by small suppliers under 

Government contracts. ' 

"In my letter of August 18, 1956, I directed the procurement agencies to implement Recommendation No. 6. In order to ensure uniformity among the various agencies the General Services Administration on December 31, 1956, laid down a governmentwide regulation prescribing policy and procedures in consonance with Recommendation No. 6. Federal agencies are taking steps to comply with this. "In its Recommendation No. 7, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That the Renegotiation Board clarify the fact that, although a contractor who subcontracts work may not reasonably expect to be allowed as large a profit thereon as if he had done the work himself, the practice of subcontracting especially the extent to which subcontracts are placed with small businesses is encouraged by giving it favorable consideration in determining allowable profits. ' "On September 24, 1956, the Renegotiation Board amended its regulations to give effect to this recommendation, "In its Recommendation No. 8, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That the life of the Small Business Administration, which is now scheduled to expire in mid-1957, be extended at the earliest opportunity.' "Administration bills (S. 1789 and H. R. 6645), would remove the time limit on the life of the Small Business Administration, thus giving it permanent status. "In its Recommendation No. 9, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That the maximum amount of an issue of corporate securities which the Securities and Exchange Commission may exempt from registration be increased from $300, 000 to $500,000. "I have recommended this change. Legislation (S. 810 and S. 843) is now before the Congress to carry out this recommendation. "In its Recommendation No. 10 the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That the President call a Conference on technical 1

research, development and distribution, for the benefit of small business.' "I have directed the Secretary of Commerce and the Administrator of the Small Business Administration to make plans for this conference. "These plans have been announced and a Conference on Technical and Distribution Research for the Benefit of Small Business will be held in Washington September 24-26. "In its Recommendation No. 11, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That legislation be enacted to enable closer Federal scrutiny of mergers. 1 "Legislation to accomplish this objective is before the Congress, and the Attorney General has outlined Administration views in testimony before the House Judiciary Committee. "In its Recommendation No. 12, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That procedural changes be made in the antitrust laws to facilitate their enforcement. ' "I have recommended three procedural changes in this area: first, that cease and desist orders of the Federal Trade

Commission under the Clayton Act be final when issued, unless appealed to the Courts; second, that the Attorney General be given the power, where civil procedures are contemplated, to issue a civil investigative demand, thus making possible the production of documents before a complaint is filed, and without the need of grand jury proceedings; third, that the Federal Trade Commission, in merger cases where it believes a violation of the law is likely, be authorized to seek a restraining injunction before filing a formal complaint. "In its Recommendation No. 13, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That wage reporting by employers for purposes of social security records and income tax withholding be simplified. ' "Legislation (H. R. 8309) to give effect to this recommendation has been submitted to the Congress. "In its Recommendation No. 14, the Cabinet Committee proposed: 'That the Office of Statistical Standards of the Bureau

of the Budget undertake a comprehensive review of the reports and statistics required of small businesses. 1 "The Bureau of the Budget has under way a study designed to determine whether the reports and statistics which small business must now maintain for, or supply to, the government are unduly burdensome and, where necessary, to suggest remedial measures.

CHAPTER II ECONOMICS OF SMALL BUSINESS 

This chapter discusses various aspects of the economic position of small business. It presents data on the indicators of the health of small business such as business population, failures and earnings; discusses Government contract awards to small business; and reviews financial factors affecting the health and growth of small business.

Business Population 

At the beginning of this year, the Nation's business population had reached an all-time high of 4, 301, 000. During 1956 there had been a net increase of 53, 800 business concerns, compared with an increase of 58, 500 firms in 1955. During the latter half of 1956, only minor changes occurred in the number of firms in operation in each of the major industry divisions. 

A total of 380, 800 new businesses were started in 1956-about the same as in 1955, when 380, 400 new concerns were established. Business discontinuances in 1956 were slightly higher than in 1955--327, 000 discontinuances in 1956 as compared with 321, 900 in 1955. An analysis by major industry division of the number of new businesses started and the number of businesses discontinued indicates a continuation of the trend toward relatively fewer manufacturers and wholesalers, and more retailers and service businesses. 

The rate of growth of contract construction firms slowed. The yearly changes in business population from 1940 through June 30, 1957, are shown in chart 1, page 10. In addition to the total number of firms in operation, the chart shows the number of new businesses started and the number of businesses discontinued and transferred during each year. "Transferred businesses' means established businesses which were transferred to new management. In 1956, a total of 327, 000 firms were purchased, reorganized or otherwise transferred to new owners. 11

New Business Incorporations 

A record number of 140, 775 new business incorporations took place in 1956. During the first half of 1957, compared with the first half of 1956, there was a decrease in incorporations to 71, 211 from 76, 257. If the decrease should continue throughout 1957, new business incorporations this year would total about 131, 500. 

To an unknown extent the number of new business incorporations reflects merely a shift in businesses from the proprietorship or partnership form to the corporate form. This, of course, lessens the value of data on incorporations. However, a significant relationship exists between new incorporations and new business formations, and figures on incorporations have the advantage of being available on a more current basis. 

During the past several years, new business incorporations in the Southern and Western States have made up an increasing proportion of the total number. This shift in the geographic distribution of new business incorporations continued during the first half of 1957. In the South Atlantic and Mountain States new business

Business Failures 

Business failures, as reported by Dun & Bradstreet,

Inc., increased during the first half of 1957. The increase was to 7, 089 from 6, 496 during the first half of 1956. This is an increase of 593, or 9 per cent. If a comparable increase occurs during the remainder of the year, business failures in 1957 would total about 13, 850. This compares with 12, 686 last year. 

Failures in the manufacturing industries during this 6months period dropped slightly. The total was 1, 176 compared with 1, 231 failures during the same period a year ago. A moderate increase in failures occurred in retail trade and commercial services, while the sharpest increase was noted in the construction industry. SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION 

The recent increase in the number of business failures has been due to several factors, such as the increased cost of doing business, keener price competition, and shifts in demand. 

For example, average weekly earnings in the retail trades (except eating and drinking places) increased from $59. 75 in May 1956 to $62. 32 in May 1957 an increase of 4. 3 per cent. Wholesale prices of finished goods increased 3. 3 per cent. But retail prices for all commodities increased only 2.7 per cent. A small reduction in the profit per dollar of a similar volume of sales results in a substantial decrease in earnings. 77 In the construction industry, the prices charged by general contractors for frame residences increased only 1 per cent from May 1956 to May 1957. On the other hand, average weekly earnings in building construction increased 5.9 per cent.

In addition, there was an increase in the wholesale prices of many building materials. Residential housing declined considerably during the first half of 1957, with housing starts 13. 3 per cent below the first half of 1956. Many contractors, familiar chiefly with residential construction, have not found it possible

to shift to other types of construction for which there is increasing demand. This drop in construction of residential housing was reflected in retail failures in the fields of lumber, building materials and hardware, and furniture and home furnishings. The increase in failures in these trades accounted for one-half of the total increase in retail failures. Chart 3 on page 14 shows the number of business failures for each of the years 1900-1956. The number of business failures also is shown on an annual basis for each month beginning with 

January 1956 through June 1957. 

Prior to 1933, business failures, as reported by Dun &

Bradstreet, Inc., included failures among agents and commercial services (such as real estate and insurance brokers, holding and finance companies, tourist agencies, etc.). These have not been included in later years, Data from 1939 through June 1957 include more complete coverage of small firms (particularly cases of voluntary discontinuances with loss to creditors, and cases of small concerns being forced out of business by such actions as attachments).

The number of business failures in 1957 apparently will be higher than in any year since prewar year 1939, when 14, 768 business concerns failed. However, in relation to the number of enterprises listed in the Dun & Bradstreet Reference Book, the rate of failures during the first half of 1957 was lower than in prewar years. According to the failure index of Dun & Bradstreet, Inc., there were 50 failures for each 10, 000 businesses in operation in the first half of 1957, compared to a failure rate of 48 per 10,000 firms last year.

This is considerably below the 70 casualties for each 10,000 businesses in 1939. Chart 4 on page 15 shows the rate of business failures for each of the years 1900-1956 and the annual rate for each month beginning with January 1956 through June 1957. 

Financial Position of Smaller Manufacturing Corporations 

During the last decade 1947-56, the gross national product in 1956 prices increased from $289.6 billion to $414.7 billion -- an increase of 42. 3 per cent. This rapid growth was interrupted briefly in 1949 and again in 1954. Between 1947 and the close of 1956, expenditures for national security, and construction of new homes and new industrial and commercial facilities, increased sharply. 

There also was a large increase in personal consumption expenditures for durable goods and services. As a result of the sharp rise in expenditures in these areas, such industries as aircraft, electrical machinery and instruments grew most rapidly, while the rate of growth of the food, textile and leather industries was more nearly the same as the rate of growth of the Nation's population. 

The effect of these changes on the smaller manufacturing corporations was to put them in a less favorable position in relation to the larger corporations than they enjoyed a decade ago. Since the latter part of 1954, the financial position of these smaller corporations has shown a marked upward trend. Nevertheless, for the decade, the gains by smaller manufacturing corporations were more than offset by their losses, and the net change was in favor of the larger corporations. 

Sales and Earnings, 1947-56 The data published by the United States Treasury Department in Statistics of Income for 1947-53 have been used to compute annual indexes of sales and of earnings before and after taxes for corporations having total assets under $1 million and for the larger corporations.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION

The increase in net sales of the smaller corporations during the period 1950-53 was not nearly as great as that of the larger corporations. Consequently, earnings of smaller corporations both before and after taxes deteriorated. Using the years 

1947-49 as base period years equaling 100, the indexes for the smaller corporations in 1953 were as follows: Net sales, 116; earnings before taxes, 76; earnings after taxes, 55. For the larger corporations, the indexes were much higher: Net sales, 156; earnings before taxes, 137; earnings after taxes, 95 (chart 5, page 17).

Statistics of income for 1954 are not available. Therefore, the data published by the Federal Trade Commission and the 

Securities and Exchange Commission in Quarterly Financial Reports for Manufacturing Corporations have been used to compute indexes for the period 1951-56. For these indexes the base year 1951 equals 100.

From 1951 to 1954, the index of net sales of the smaller corporations declined to 85, while that of the larger corporations increased to 104. As a result of an upward trend in sales during 

1955 and 1956, the index for the smaller corporations had increased to 111 in 1956, but this was still well below the index of 128 for the larger corporations. 

The earnings before taxes of the smaller corporations dropped from the base year figure of 100 in 1951 to 50 in 1954, then rose sharply to 91 in 1956. In the case of the larger corporations, a decline to 78 in 1954 was followed by an increase to 109 in 1956. 

The trend of earnings after taxes of the smaller corporations was similar to that of earnings before taxes. A drop from 100 in 1951 to 55 in 1954 was followed by an increase to 110 in 1956. 

But for the larger corporations, there was a decline from 100 in 1951 to 89 in 1952, followed by an increase to 136 in 1956 (chart 6, page 19). 

Sales and Earnings for 6 Months Ending March 31, 1957

Sales of the smaller manufacturing corporations during the 6 months ending March 31, 1957, were a little higher than during the 6 months ending March 31, 1956. The sales of corporations having total assets under $1 million were $21, 478 million, compared with $20, 128 million a year ago. On the other hand, the sales of the

This substantial increase was due to higher sales of corporations engaged in petroleum refining and the manufacture of aircraft, motor vehicles, and machinery. These are industries characterized by a small number of relatively large corporations. Sales volume decreased in industries manufacturing lumber and wood products; stone, clay and glass products; and textile mill products. 

Earnings before taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations during the 6 months ending March 31, 1957, were $665 million compared with $658 million in the corresponding quarters a year earlier. 

Earnings after taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations were $317 million during the 6 months ending March 31, 1957, compared with $325 million in the comparable 

6 months a year earlier.

The table on page 21 shows earnings before and after taxes by asset size of manufacturing corporation starting with the second quarter of 1955 and continuing through the first quarter of 1957. Beginning with the first quarter of 1956 the estimates of sales and earnings have been based on a new sample. The amount of earnings estimated on the basis of the old sample or the new sample is specified to permit comparisons with comparable data to the extent possible. New sample estimates of the earnings of the smaller manufacturing corporations are appreciably higher than those based on the old sample.

Rate of Return on Stockholders' Equity 

In 1956, after taxes, the rate of earnings on stockholders' equity in the smaller corporations was 10.4 per cent, compared with 12. 4 per cent for the larger corporations. 

During the years 1950-53, the average rates of return were 9. 1 and 12.4 per cent, respectively. The rate of earnings after taxes in the smaller corporations on stockholders' equity was 6.8 per cent for the 6 months ending March 31, 1957, compared with 7.2 per cent in the comparable two quarters a year earlier.

Chart 7 shows the annual rates of earnings on stockholders' equity both before and after taxes, by size of corporation, for the decade 1947-56.

The financial position of the smaller manufacturing corporations during the past decade may be better than indicated in the preceding paragraphs. There are several reasons for this: 1. 2. 

Some manufacturing corporations which had under $1 million of total assets in the earlier years have grown and are now included in the statistics for the larger corporations. 

The greater increases in the indexes of sales and earnings of the larger corporations are partly due to the more rapid growth of industries where the larger corporations are concentrated. 

Further, these industries are the ones whose earnings have been historically higher. To this extent, therefore, changes in the indexes do not reflect a change in the financial position of the smaller manufacturing corporations in their respective industries. 

3. The Federal Trade Commission and the 

Securities and Exchange Commission estimates based on the new sample indicate that the sales and earnings of the smaller corporations were higher than the estimates based on the old sample. 4. 

There was a substantial increase in officers' salaries during the decade.

Analysis of the Statistics of Income data shows that the amount of officers' salaries plus earnings after taxes as reported by the smaller corporations did not decrease significantly between 

1950 and 1953.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION

Change in Position of Small Retail Establishments 

1948 and 1954

The Bureau of the Census has now released statistics regarding the employment size of retail establishments included in the 

1954 Census of Business. The Small Business Administration has reviewed the statistics for 21 selected kinds of retail trade to determine changes in the status of smaller stores from the 1948 to the 

1954 Census period. These 21 retail trades accounted for 77.2 per cent of the total stores and 77. 8 per cent of the total sales of all establishments included in the 1954 Census of Retail Trade. 

In general there was an increase in the percentage of total sales accounted for by the smallest retail stores with no paid employees. On the other hand, the smaller retail stores having 1 or more paid employees but fewer than 20 did not maintain their position in some kinds of retail business. This was the case for grocery stores and passenger car dealers. See table on page 25.

Analysis by employment size for this comparison is based upon the size classification of establishments -- not companies. In some of the retail trades, many of the smaller stores (those with fewer than 20 employees) are owned by chains. Thus, a change in the percentage of sales made by the smaller stores may not accurately reflect the change in position of the smaller independently owned and operated stores.

In relation to the last Census of Retail Trade (1948), the 1954 Census was based upon different methods of enumeration, classification and tabulation. In 1954 data were obtained largely through a mail canvass based upon Social Security and Internal Revenue Service records rather than by personal enumeration at the place of business. In 1948 all establishments having sales of $500 or more were included, whereas in 1954 stores with no payroll (during entire year) were excluded if their sales were less than $2,500. In 1948 "lumber yards" and "building materials dealers" that sold primarily to builders and general contractors were classified as wholesale trade, whereas in 1954 these establishments were included in the Census of Retail Trade.

Military Procurement 

Total expenditures for national security have fluctuated widely since the beginning of World War II. In 1939 national security expenditures of $1, 258 million were only 1.4 per cent of the gross national product. In 1944 these expenditures increased to a peak of $88, 615 million, or 41.9 per cent. After the war, they dropped rapidly and in 1947 were $13, 349 million, or 5.8 per cent of the gross national product. Again in the Korean period, they reached a peak of $52, 022 million, or 14. 2 per cent of the gross national product. Since that time, national defense expenditures have declined to about 10 per cent. Thus, the impact of military procurement on small business decreased significantly immediately after World War II and is not as great today as it was during the Korean period.

Although net military prime contracts decreased from 15.2 per cent of total manufacturers' sales in 1952 to 5. 4 per cent in 1956, these contracts account for a much higher percentage of sales in some industries. This would be particularly true in the industries making aircraft, ships, guided missiles, and products for use in electronics and communications. Furthermore, military prime contracts have become more concentrated in industries characterized by big enterprises operating big plants. Estimated expenditures for aircraft, including associated armament and electronics, were 58 per cent of major defense procurement and production in fiscal year 1957, compared with less than 30 per cent in fiscal 1951 and 1952. Expenditures for guided missiles increased from less than 1 per cent in fiscal year 1951 to 13 per cent in fiscal year 1957. Expenditures for military public works and for research and development also have increased. These changes in the amount of national security expenditures, in the proportion of such expenditures to the gross national product, and in the composition of major defense procurement and production, have reduced the opportunities for small business to share in military procurement.

The result is a needed shifting in the emphasis of Government programs designed to strengthen the competitive position of small business.

The effectiveness of small business procurement assistance programs cannot be evaluated merely by studying changes in the small business percentage of total net military contract awards, since the character of the shift in procurement makes less procurement suitable for small business than for large business.

Small Business Share of Net Procurement 

During the period July 1956 through May 1957, the total net value of all military prime contract awards increased to $16, 824 million compared with $13, 383 million during the same months of fiscal year 1956. Prime contract awards to small business firms increased to $3, 175 million, compared with $2, 791 million in the same period of last fiscal year and $2, 583 million 2 years ago. 

Awards to small business were higher for each military service. 

The increase in the amount of small business prime contracts by military services is shown in chart 8. 

The over-all small business percentage decreased to 18.9 from 20. 9 per cent of the total net value of military prime contracts awarded. This decrease was spread between the three military services and may be explained at least in part by changes in the composition of military procurement within each of the services. 

Small Business Share of New Procurement 

Over a long period of time, data on the small business share of net procurement, discussed above, may provide a better measure of small business participation in defense purchasing than statistics on new procurement. This is because price reductions or scheduled cutbacks, which are taken into account in determining net procurement, affect ultimate payment of funds to large business concerns to a greater extent than to small business firms. 

However, new military procurement figures, which do not include such cancellations, may provide better information than net procurement data for determining how military prime contracts are currently being awarded to small business. The table on page 30 shows the value of contracts awarded, on both a new and net military procurement basis, for the months of July through May of fiscal years 1956 and 1957. 

This table also includes the amount of the small business share of procurement for each military service and the Military Petroleum Supply Agency. The table on page 31 gives the percentage distribution of total military procurement by size of business and by military service.

The trend of contract awards to small business in relation to total contract awards for both new and net procurement,

beginning with the first quarter of fiscal year 1954 and continuing through the third quarter of fiscal year 

1957, is shown in chart 9, page 33.

The higher small business percentage for advertised procurement is due almost entirely to the greater portion of advertised procurement within the small business potential.

Financial Factors Affecting Loan Applications 

The demand for private bank loans continues to be strong, and the amount of loans outstanding increased during the first 6 months of the year. The rate of expansion, however, has slowed down. Compared with an increase of $2.9 billion in business loans outstanding of commercial banks during the first 6 months of 1956, the increase in the first 6 months of 1957 was down almost 50 per cent to $1.5 billion.

The tightness of credit in early 1957, as during the whole of 1956, was due mainly to the extraordinarily high level. of business activity with a demand for funds for inventory accumulation and capital expansion. Business expenditures for new plant and equipment of $18. 2 billion in the first half of 1957 are up almost $2 billion compared to the same period of 1956. Easing of demand has been pronounced in the housing industry where there has been a reduction in new housing starts. Demand also has fallen off in some industrial sectors. Metal manufacturers borrowed only two-thirds as much this year as in the first 5 months of 1956, and petroleum and chemical companies borrowed only one-third as much.

Administration.

Interest rates generally have moved upward in response to the rise in the prime rate charged to the best corporate risks of 4 per cent. The recent increase to 4 per cent in the interest rate paid by the Government in its refinancing operations may foreshadow some further upward revision in the prime rate and in the rates charged generally on business loans. This upward trend in business rates has brought the Small Business Administration rate of 6 per cent in closer alignment with prevailing interest rates or in some cases has made it a more attractive rate. 

The slowing up in the expansion of private bank credit has not been reflected in a slowup in the demand for small business loans, in view of bulging bank loan portfolios for certain types of loans and intense demand from larger businesses. The increasing number of loan applications to the Small Business Administration may suggest that a growing number of businesses of good credit standing, unable to obtain adequate financing through normal banking channels because credit demands have been outrunning credit supply, have turned for financial assistance to the Federal Government. 

Equity and Long-Term Capital for Small Business 

Security flotations to obtain capital have offset the slowing up in the rate of increase of bank borrowing by industry. However, smaller security flotations have shown a strikingly opposite trend, with a decreasing use of Regulation A, through which the Securities and Exchange Commission provides a simplified registration procedure for issues between $100, 000 and $300, 000 in size. 

Corporate securities offerings during the period January May 1957 were higher than during the same months of 1956. The increase in the total was from $4, 346 million to $5, 285 million. 

There was a similar increase in offerings of equity securities (common and preferred stocks) from $990 million to $1,264 million. 

On the other hand, a significant decrease occurred in offerings of securities (stocks and bonds) pursuant to Regulation A from $86 million to $49 million. 

For the period January- May 1957, public offerings of securities issues from $100, 000 to $300, 000 decreased to 0.9 per cent of total corporate offerings, compared to 2.0 per cent in 

January-May 1956. For the year 1956, the percentage was 1.6 per cent compared with 2.6 per cent in 1955.

United States Securities and Exchange Commission 

The slump in mining issues, particularly the disappearance of the uranium boom, is partly responsible for the reduction in Regulation A issues. A tightening up of Securities and Exchange Commission requirements, and the impact of inflation in raising the cost of floating small issues while diminishing the utility of the amount left for the small company after paying these higher costs also undoubtedly contributed to reduced use of Regulation A.

Development Credit Corporations 

The spread of development credit corporations both local and statewide has been notable in recent months. It is estimated that there are many hundreds of local pools of capital operating in one way or another to increase industrial development and payrolls in their areas.

Development credit corporations at the State level are now active in Connecticut, Maine, Massachusetts, New York,

New Hampshire, North Carolina and Rhode Island. Corporations are authorized in six other States but are not currently engaged in lending operations. These are Arkansas, Georgia, Michigan, South Dakota, Vermont and Wisconsin. Seven States are considering specific legislative proposals for development credit corporations: Florida, Minnesota, Mississippi, Montana, New Jersey,

South Carolina and West Virginia. Six other States are actively considering formation of such corporations, but the proposals have not yet reached the legislative stage. These are Arizona, Illinois, New Mexico, Oregon, Virginia and Washington. Vermont has an authorized State corporation which will be ready to operate after raising additional capital. Kansas has a corporation which is on a regional basis. Pennsylvania has a credit corporation which differs from the others in that it uses State rather than private funds. Some of the established corporations have been extremely active in assisting with the credit needs of small firms in their States.

CHAPTER III 

FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE 

During the period January 1 through June 30, 1957, business loans by the Small Business Administration again increased sharply. In this 6-months period, the Agency approved 

1,936 business loans for $89, 035, 954, compared with 1,600 business loan approvals for $70, 058, 890 during the previous period, and 1, 290 business loan approvals totaling $51, 644, 868 during the first half of 1956. 

In the Disaster Loan Program, during the 6-months period the Administrator declared 40 disaster areas in 21 States and the Territory of Hawaii. The disasters resulted from tornadoes, excessive rainfall, snowslides, tidal waves, floods and hurricanes. The Agency received 1, 456 disaster loan applications compared with 1, 435 during the same months of 1956. It approved 1,319 disaster loans aggregating $10, 360, 503 to assist in rehabilitating businesses and homes. 

As a part of its Disaster Loan Program the Small Business Administration has placed special emphasis on financial assistance to concerns which have suffered economic injury as a result of drought conditions. Banks in the drought areas have agreed to assist in this program by accepting drought loan applications for the Agency and by obtaining credit information needed in considering loan requests. Arrangements also have been made for local advisory committees composed of bankers and businessmen to assist in evaluating drought loan applications.

Drought disaster loan approvals in the 6-months period totaled 98 for $1,670,680, of which the Small Business Administration share was $1, 634, 026. In the previous 6-months period, the Small Business Administration approved 12 drought loans for $381,000, of which the Agency's share was $378, 305. Business and disaster loan approvals during fiscal year 1957 totaled 5, 133 for $172, 087, 000, compared with 5, 224 approvals for $126, 379, 000 during fiscal year 1956. 39

From the beginning of its Financial Assistance Program in October 1953 through June 30, 1957, the Small Business Administration had approved 13, 245 small business and disaster loans for $390, 795, 000. This total includes 7, 096 business loans for $324, 786, 000 and 6, 149 disaster loans for $66, 009, 000.

The continued increase in business loan applications and approvals apparently is due to these factors: 

1. The high level of business activity has resulted in an enormous demand for credit. Although bank credit has expanded to meet this demand, the volume of loan applications coming to the Small Business Administration continues at an increased rate. 

2. The favorable acceptance of the Small Business Administration's Limited Loan Participation Plan, under which the Agency will join with a bank in a loan to a small firm up to a maximum of $15,000 in Government funds, or 75 per cent of the loan, whichever is the smaller. This plan is especially designed to meet the credit needs of small retail, wholesale, and service firms. Currently, about 37 per cent of the business loan applications approved by the Agency are for loans of this type. 

3. During the past year the Small Business Administration's Loan Policy Board extended loan eligibility to certain types of firms which previously were excluded from the loan program. 4. 

Banks are steadily growing better acquainted with the Small Business Administration's programs and services, and are calling upon the Agency to help with the credit needs of the small firms they serve. Because of the unanticipated increase in demand for business loans, the Small Business Administration found it necessary to ask Congress for an increase in the statutory limitation of $150 million on business loans outstanding. Public Law 85-4, approved February 11, 1957, raised this limitation to $230 million. 

This is in addition to the $125 million limitation on disaster loans. 

As of June 30, 1957, the balance of the business loan limitation of $230 million was $41.7 million. Of the $125 million legislative limitation on Small Business Administration disaster loans, there was a balance of $77. 7 million.

Due to the increased volume of loan requests and approvals, the Small Business Administration also found it necessary early this year to request a supplemental appropriation to increase the capital of the Agency's revolving fund which finances the lending activities. An appropriation of $45 million was provided for this purpose in the Urgent Deficiency Appropriation Act, approved April 20, 1957. 

Pending action on the appropriation request, the shortage of funds and the unknown demand for disaster loans resulting from floods in Kentucky, Virginia and West Virginia, required a halt in approval of business loans as of February 1, 1957. The Agency's field offices were instructed to inform applicants whose loans would be approved that their loans had been given favorable consideration but that formal approval could not be given until funds became available. Accordingly, except to meet emergency situations, no business loans were formally approved during the more than 2-1/2 months period from February 1 until April 22, 1957.

Of the total of $220 million appropriated by Congress to the revolving fund, $4. 1 million remained uncommitted and thus available for lending purposes, as of June 30, 1957.

Financial Counseling The Small Business Administration's financial special ists, who have had long experience in credit and financial matters, devote many hours to reviewing the financial problems of small business owners and managers.

Frequently, these specialists find that an Agency loan is not the real solution to a businessman's problem, that what is needed is an increase in capitalization of the business, liquidation of surplus inventory and obsolete equipment, revision of sales or product policy, or some similar step. If However, it is the policy of the Small Business Administration not to discourage the filing of any application for a loan. it appears that a loan is not the solution to a businessman's financial problem, or that his application would not meet reasonable credit standards, he is advised of this. Nevertheless, if the businessman wishes to apply for a loan the Small Business Administration will accept his application and give it earnest consideration.

The Small Business Administration plans to give increased assistance to small business concerns in finding sources of equity capital through the securities market. The Agency's field offices have been given lists of qualified investment banking houses in their areas which have expressed an interest in assisting smaller firms raise equity money. These lists can be referred to by small businesses interested in obtaining equity capital through the issuance of securities. 

Loan Policy

In the last 6-months period, the Small Business Administration, through action of its Loan Policy Board, has extended loan eligibility to small businesses engaged in the practice of a profession or rendering professional services. The Agency also has decided to consider applications for financial assistance submitted by technical and trade schools, including business schools which are privately owned and operated for profit. The same credit criteria will apply to these applications as to other business loan applications. 

During this reporting period, the Agency continued its co-operation with other agencies in loan programs. The Atomic 

Energy Community Act of 1955 provides for the disposition by the Atomic-Energy Commission of the Government-owned properties located at Oak Ridge, Tennessee, and Richland, Washington. Subsequently, by Executive Order the President transferred to the 

Administrator of the Housing and Home Finance Agency certain responsibilities in connection with the sale and financing of properties under this Act. The Administrator of the Housing and Home 

Finance Agency has established a Community Disposition Program to carry out these responsibilities.

As part of this program, the Small Business Administration and the Housing and Home Finance Agency have entered into an agreement under which financial specialists of the Small Business Administration will process all applications for commercial financing. In those cases where applicants qualify for financial assistance under the Small Business Act of 1953, the Small Business Administration will itself consider extending the loans. 

Where applicants are not eligible for financial assistance from the Small Business Administration, officials in charge of the Community Disposition Program will determine whether to furnish financial assistance to the applicants.

The Small Business Administration continued its co-operation with the Fish and Wildlife Service, U. S. 

Department of the Interior, in connection with the financing and refinancing of operations, maintenance, replacement, repair and equipment of fishing gear and vessels; and of financing research into the basic problems of fisheries. In this connection, Small Business Administration processes, disburses and services loans approved by the Secretary of Interior where funds have been made available by the fisheries loan fund established by Section 4 of the Fish and Wildlife Act of 1956 (70 Stat. 1119, 1121). To date the Agency has processed 204 applications under this program. 

Administration of Business Loan Program amount. 

To speed up action on loan applications, Small

Business Administration regional directors have been delegated authority to decline initial applications and applications for reconsideration of loans (direct or participation) in any Before an application can be declined, however, the regional director, the financial specialist who processed the application and the Chief of Financial Assistance Division. 

must unanimously agree to this action. If the applicant is dissatisfied with the reasons for the declination or specifically requests further reconsideration, the application then is submitted to the Washington office.

Experience has shown that better appraisals are needed both for loan purposes and insurance protection. It is now the Agency's policy to make its own appraisal of collateral, in order to determine the soundness of a proposed loan and to check the adequacy of hazard insurance on the collateral.

In order to insure, insofar as possible, that these loans will be repaid, and to achieve more efficient operations, financial specialists charged with loan administration have been organized into a Loan Administration and Liquidation Section in the larger regional offices. It will be the responsibility of the specialists to see that every outstanding loan in their regions is given continuing attention and that repayments are received when due. Emphasis is being placed on preventive action before a loan becomes delinquent rather than waiting until the delinquency occurs.

In order to make its financial assistance and other services more readily available to many small firms and to meet the demands for its services, the

Small Business Adminis tration has recently opened several new branch and suboffices of the regional offices. 

The Agency also has started several additional "circuit rider" operations whereby a financial specialist is assigned to a specific community one or more days each week to assist small firms with their financial problems.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION The volume of business loan requests has reached a point almost double the 350 applications per month which had been anticipated by the Agency and provided for in its budget for the fiscal year 1957. 

During the 6-months period, January 1 - June 30, 1957, the Small Business Administration received 3, 527 business loan applications. During the same months of 1956, the Agency received 

2, 578 applications.

The sharply increased demand for business loans is indicated in chart 10 above which shows the number of applications received during fiscal years 1955, 1956 and 1957. During the period January-June 1957, the number of applications reached a high of 702 in the month of March, with a low of 496 in January. 

During the period January-June 1956, applications reached a high of 514 in June and were between 267 and 505 in the other months. 

Bank Participation Loans 

Of the 1, 936 business loans approved in the 6-months period, January-June 1957, 1, 343, or 69. 4 per cent, were bank

participation loans. The proportion of participation and direct loans is shown on chart 11, page 46.

In the January-June 1957 period, there also was a marked change in the nature of the participations, with a greater number of banks asking the Agency to participate on an immediate basis, rather than a deferred one. This trend results in an outgo of monies from the Agency's revolving fund at a faster rate. The percentage of deferred participation loans dropped to 18. 1 per cent of total business loan approvals in the last 6 months of the fiscal . year compared to 24. 2 per cent in the previous 6 months, while the percentage of immediate participation loans increased from 48. 0 to 51.3 per cent. During the fiscal year, the percentage of deferred participation loans dropped from 31. 8 to 20.9 per cent of all business loan approvals, while the percentage of immediate participation loans increased from 41. 1 to 49. 7 per cent. This shift to immediate participation loans undoubtedly reflects the desire of the banks to use their loan funds to extend credit to as many of their customers as possible. It reflects also the relative unprofitability to banks of deferred participation loans in view of the prevailing higher interest rates now charged for bank loans as contrasted with the interest rate remaining to a bank on a deferred participation loan after the Small Business Administration's participation fee is deducted. 

In the period October 1953 through June 30, 1957, business loans in which banks participated totaled $233, 768,000, of which the Small Business Administration's share was $178, 958, 000.

Limited Loan Participation Program 

During the period January-June 1957, loans under this program accounted for about 1 out of every 3 business loans approved. 

Under this loan plan, the Agency will participate with a bank in a loan to a small firm up to a maximum of $15,000, or 75 per cent of the loan, whichever is the lesser. Maximum maturity is 5 years, and the maximum interest rate is 6 per cent per annum. 

During fiscal year 1957, the Small Business Administration approved 1, 307 loans for $19, 456, 000 under the Limited Loan

Participation Program. During fiscal year 1956, it had approved 

519 loans of this type for $7,311, 000. During the last 6 months, the 

Agency approved 667 of these loans for $9, 699, 000, compared with 

640 loans for $9, 757, 000 in the previous 6-months period.

On a cumulative basis, more than 67 per cent of these limited loans have been to retail, wholesale and service establishments, compared with 47 per cent of all Agency business loans to these industries. An analysis by industry of the loans approved under this program is shown below:

Loan Declinations 

Frequently, loan applications are declined for two or more reasons. Analysis of the 1, 355 applications declined during this reporting period shows that applications were most frequently disapproved because earning ability was not demonstrated or assured for the future. However, this was the reason in relatively fewer cases than during the previous 6 months. Similarly, insufficient equity investment (including disproportionate debt-net worth ratio) was less often the reason for declining applications. On the other hand, there was a relative increase in the number of business loan applications declined because of insufficient collateral, unsatisfactory financial condition, or because need for Small Business Administration funds was not demonstrated. 

An analysis of loan declinations by the Small Business 

Administration during the last 6-months period follows:

Loans by Industry 

Loan applications from small manufacturers continue to be high in relation to the number of manufacturers in the total business population. Of the 1, 936 business loans approved by the 

Agency during the last 6 months, 617 or almost one-third were to small manufacturers whereas manufacturers make up only between 7 and 8 per cent of the total business population. 

Furthermore, loans to small manufacturers may continue to be a higher percentage of business loan approvals than the number of manufacturers would suggest because of their relatively greater need for funds required to make the capital expenditures essential to expansion and growth.

Disaster Loans The Small Business Administration is authorized by section 207 (b) of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended, to make loans to assist in the rehabilitation of homes and businesses which have been damaged or destroyed by floods, storms or other natural disasters. The Agency also is authorized to make loans to businesses which are located within an area designated as a drought disaster area by the President or the Secretary of Agriculture, and which have suffered economic loss as a result of the drought. The Agency is prepared to assist businesses suffering economic loss as a result of drought conditions whenever there is a reasonable chance of rehabilitation,

Disbursements and Commitments As of June 30, 1957, disbursements had been made on 5,023 of the 6, 062 net approved business loans after cancellation -- 

82.9 per cent of net approvals.

Disbursements, naturally, lag behind the volume of loan approvals. Usually, the borrower himself controls the interval between the approval and disbursement of an authorized loan. When delay occurs, it may be due to difficulty in starting construction of facilities or obtaining machinery and equipment; flaws in a real estate title; the death of a key employee; an adverse change in the borrower's condition; or other reasons. The Small Business

Administration on its part is continuing to give special attention to methods and procedures to achieve a closer follow-up with borrowers to assist them in meeting terms and conditions. 

As of June 30, 1957, disbursements made by banks or the Small Business Administration on business loans amounted to $216,612,000, out of total net commitments to borrowers of $278, 326, 000. The Small Business Administration's share of this net commitment amount was $230, 999, 000.

In the case of disaster loans, total net commitments to borrowers were $57,606, 000. The Small Business Administration share was $55,083,000. Disbursements totaled $52,364, 000. 

Consequently, the total amount of net commitments to all borrowers (business and disaster loans) was $335, 932, 000, of which the Small Business Administration share was $286, 082,000. 

Sale of Loans 

About two years ago, the Small Business Administration began to place on sale its seasoned loans. To date, 75 loans in an authorized amount of $3,974, 300, have been sold to banks or refinanced through them. No loan is offered for sale by the Agency unless it has been disbursed for a 6-months period or longer.

In all cases the borrower's bank of deposit is given the first opportunity to purchase the loan. This is in line with the Agency's policy of trying to help the small business concern establish a normal bank-customer relationship. It is also in keeping with the 

Congressional intent that the Small Business Administration should not compete with private lending institutions. 

Cancellations 

Of the 7, 096 business loans approved since start of the

Small Business Administration's lending program, 1, 034 have been canceled in full prior to disbursement. The principal reasons for cancellation have been: 1. 

Borrower made other arrangements for funds; 2. 

Borrower found he could get along without the loan; 

3. Borrower was unable or unwilling to comply with loan conditions; 

4. Borrower's financial condition deteriorated to the extent that the loan could not be disbursed.

Delinquencies and Charge-Offs 

As previously stated, disbursement has been made on 5, 023 business loans in an amount of $216, 612,000. Of these, 80 loans are in a liquidation status and 105 have payments due which are delinquent in excess of 60 days. On these 185 loans the unpaid principal balance is $5, 745, 842, and the amount thereof delinquent (including the full unpaid principal on "liquidation" loans) is $3, 142, 718.

However, in most instances there has been adequate collateral and liquidation actions have thus far resulted in the charge-off by the Small Business Administration of only $97, 845 of principal on 8 loans. With respect to the total disbursements of $52,364,000 on 

4,849 disaster loans, borrowers were delinquent in excess of 60 days on 264 loans with an unpaid principal of $2, 154, 410. 

The amount

delinquent was $635, 299. Nine loans have been charged off by the 

Small Business Administration with a principal amount involved of $17,450.

In the administration of Reconstruction Finance Corporation disaster loans transferred to Small Business Administration on July 1, 1954, for liquidation, the number of delinquent loans stands at one of the lowest points since Small Business Administration took over their liquidation. As of the reporting date of May 25, 1957, there were 230 loans with a total principal balance of $668, 777 past due for 61 days or more as against 233 on November 25, 1956, with a balance in the reserve transferred from Reconstruction Finance 

Corporation of $1, 130, 200. Total write-offs since July 1, 1954, have amounted to only $69, 991.

CHAPTER IV GOVERNMENT PROCUREMENT ASSISTANCE 

During the past 6 months, progress was made in each of the Small Business Administration's programs to assist small business firms desiring to sell to the Government. Of particular significance was a sharp increase in the amount of Government purchases jointly set aside for award to small business concerns despite the fact that the percentage of total Government procurement suitable for award to small business decreased. 

Highlights of the progress made during the last 6 months of fiscal year 1957 as compared to the last half of fiscal year 1956 are: 436936 O-57-5 1. 2. 3. 4. 5. 

The number of Government procurements set aside for award to small business, through joint action by the Small Business Administration and Government purchasing agencies, increased from 3, 904 to 7,062, or 80.9 per cent.

The number of contract awards resulting from these set-asides increased from 

4, 435 to 8, 169, or 84. 2 per cent.

The dollar value of the procurements set aside for small business increased from $304, 864, 565 to $399, 060, 849, or 30.9 per cent. 

The dollar value of contract awards resulting from the set-asides during this period increased from $187, 067, 429 to $334, 923, 189, or 79. 0 per cent. The number of calls on prime contractors and large subcontractors to develop 61

6. 7. 8. subcontracting opportunities for small business increased from 2, 006 to 2, 384, or 18.8 per cent.

The number of referrals of subcontracting opportunities increased from 1,806 to 

2, 997, or 65.9 per cent.

The number of firms provided procurement assistance counseling or representation at purchasing installations increased from 12, 752 to 15, 003, or 17.7 per cent.

The number of cases of aid to small firms in obtaining the opportunity to bid on specific purchases increased from 3, 231 to 4, 884, or 51. 2 per cent.

Joint Set-Asides for Small Business 

The Small Business Administration and the major purchasing agencies of the Government have a co-operative program under which suitable proposed Government purchases are set aside, or reserved, for award to small business concerns. 

Through the set-aside program small business firms are given the opportunity to compete with other small businesses for Government purchases which have been earmarked exclusively for small firms. By increasing the number of items which may be restricted to small business competition and, at the same time, increasing the number of small business bidders and suppliers on these items, the program serves to broaden the procurement base. 

To carry out the set-aside program, the Small Business Administration has assigned representatives to certain major Government purchasing offices. These representatives review proposed purchases, determine the types of facilities needed to produce the items, evaluate the prospects for small business competition, and advise procurement officials of those purchases which they believe should be earmarked for competition exclusively among small firms. 

Either an entire purchase, or a portion of it, can be set aside exclusively for small business competition when the Government purchasing agency agrees to this action. Where only part of

fiscal year 1954, there has been a 237.4 per cent increase in the value of purchases set aside for small business concerns. 

During fiscal year 1957, 11, 851 procurements amounting to $744, 335, 298 were reserved for small business. This is an increase of 95 per cent in number, and almost 50 per cent in dollar value of set-asides when compared to fiscal year 1956. 

Since the start of the Small Business Administration's operations on August 1, 1953, 23, 471 Government purchases totaling $1,857, 128, 678 have been reserved for exclusive award to small business.

The table on page 67 provides a breakdown of results for the past 6 months for each Department or Agency with which the Small Business Administration has an active joint set-aside program. 

Although the program with the Department of Commerce was begun only last November, the results have been especially gratifying. The purchasing activities of the Departments of Agriculture and Interior are so widely decentralized that Small Business Administration representatives are able to cover only a small percentage of these agencies' purchasing offices. 

A recent change in military procurement instructions pertaining to partial small business set-asides should be beneficial to small firms. In the future when purchase of the unreserved quantities has resulted in multiple awards at different unit prices, awards for the set-aside quantities shall be made at the highest unit price of the unreserved quantities. Formerly, awards on the setaside portion were made at a unit price no higher than the weighted average of awards on the unrestricted portion of the procurement. 

This often meant that, in order to receive an award under the setaside part of the purchase, a small firm had to agree to a unit price which was lower than the prices bid by some successful large bidders on the unreserved part of the procurement. This change in awards policy has been urged for some time by the Small Business 

Administration.
