Business Administration representatives learn of items for which additional small business competition is needed. They then advise selected Agency field offices of these items and of specific bidding opportunities and arrange for the offices to receive related specifications and drawings. These offices, in turn, carefully select small firms in their areas which are competent to produce the items or furnish the services needed, and notify them of the opportunities to compete. The firms which wish to bid are brought to the attention of purchasing officials and arrangements made for them to receive bid sets on the next purchase. In this way, Small Business Administration representatives are able to broaden the base of supply, and often to have procurements set aside for small business concerns on which there had been previously but little small business competition. 

Here are a few of the items or services on which Small Business Administration representatives were able to secure joint set-asides for small business during the past 6 months through locating additional suppliers: Development of radical sizing system for cold weather combat clothing; milk; generators; ice cream; prefabricated bridges; prefabricated buildings; cotton sheets; custodial services; tablecloths; napkins; indicator lights for airfield runways; panelboard, power distribution, amplifiers; antenna assemblies; general construction radio range communication centers; office supplies; paper items; men's overcoats; water tanks, 10-gauge steel; corrugated galvanized pipe; sprinkler system installation; foundation for high voltage machine; replacing and repiping main branch steam line; dining tables; fluorescent fixtures; reroofing; cleaning brushes for guns; paint.

Small Business Administration field offices also arrange with local purchasing offices of Federal agencies (offices not covered by Small Business Administration representatives) to furnish the local buyers in these offices the names of potential small business suppliers of items for which additional competition is needed.

During the past 6 months, Agency staff members advised 4, 925 small firms of Government purchases of items suitable to their facilities and for which small business suppliers were needed. 

Procurement Assistance Counseling 

The Small Business Administration helps small firms determine which items or services they can supply to the Government 15 Cove lec era pec st

and which agencies buy them, and assists the firms in obtaining listing on the appropriate bidders lists. In addition, the 

Agency helps small business firms obtain bid sets for purchases which they are able to supply. 11 A basic objective of the Agency's counseling program is to provide small firms the fullest possible information about Government purchasing. Toward this end, the Agency has published the "U. S. Government Purchasing Directory, the "U. S. Government Specifications Directory," and "Pointers on Government Contracting. " These publications provide detailed information on the items and services bought by the Federal Government and the agencies which buy them, sources of specifications used by the agencies in their purchasing, and steps to take in locating prime contract and subcontract opportunities.

Many Small Business Administration field offices obtain bid sets for suitable Government purchases and display them for informational purposes. The offices inform interested firms as to how copies of the bid sets and specifications and drawings relating to them can be obtained. 

The Small Business Administration and the Interna tional Cooperation Administration are co-operating to provide small firms information on export trade opportunities. The 

International Cooperation Administration furnishes Small Busi ness Administration regional and branch offices "Small Business Circulars" containing information on export trade opportunities for small business under the programs financed by the Interna tional Cooperation Administration. Small Business Administration field offices use these circulars to inform interested small manufacturers, suppliers, and exporters of needed items, quantities, specifications, shipping instructions, and names and addresses of purchasing offices. The Small Business Administration also assists small firms in solving problems regarding bids, specifications, and materials requirements, and helps resolve disputes between small firms and Government procurement officials. During the 6 months ending June 30, 1957, the Agency's field offices reported 15, 003 cases of general procurement counseling and representation in behalf of small firms at purchasing offices, as compared to 12, 752 for the like period of 1956.

Prime Contract Referral Program 

When a small business firm requests Small Business Administration assistance in obtaining Government orders and contracts, it is asked to provide detailed information regarding its production facilities. This information is analyzed by the 

Agency's procurement specialists, and the firm then is notified of current procurement opportunities which are suited to its particular type of equipment, and advised how to get on the bidders lists to receive future bidding opportunities direct from the purchasing installations. Since the Agency's field offices obtain bid sets from the principal Government purchasing offices, they are able to notify small firms of many bidding opportunities of which the firms otherwise would be unaware. On 

During the past 6 months, 23, 890 notifications of current bidding opportunities were made to small business firms which had requested such assistance. Statistics on awards resulting from prime contract referrals have been discontinued because of the amount of time required to obtain the data. the basis of past data, however, this program is a very effective aid to small firms. For instance, during the only period for which data are available July 1954 through June 1956 awards to small firms as the result of referrals amounted to $579, 491, 000.

Subcontracting Program 

11 17 Small firms which request subcontract assistance from the Small Business Administration are asked to furnish detailed information concerning their plant facilities, equipment, and items which they can make or services they can provide. After analyzing this information, Agency representatives develop subcontracting opportunities for the small firms with prime contractors and large subcontractors. Small firm representatives are counseled on how to sell their services to prime contractors and how to find firms needing components which they can produce. In addition, the small businesses are provided information on major prime contractors in their areas, including the Atomic Energy Commission's cost-type contractors and, where appropriate, construction prime contractors for the Army Corps of Engineers and Navy District Public Works 

Offices.

The Small Business Administration coordinates its pro gram with that of the military services by communicating with those defense prime contractors who have agreed to subcontract to small business concerns to the maximum extent practicable. However, the Agency's efforts to develop subcontract opportunities are not limited to these large military prime contractors. Agency representatives also call on small prime contractors and often are able to bring to their attention competent complementary facilities. 

The Atomic Energy Commission and the Small Business 

Administration have a co-operative subcontracting program to aid small business concerns. Small Business Administration field offices maintain liaison with the Atomic Energy Commission's costtype contractors in order to learn of opportunities for small firms to subcontract or to sell to them.

The Small Business Administration staff members called upon 2, 384 prime contractors during the past 6 months to develop subcontracting work for small concerns. As a result, 2,997 referrals of subcontracting opportunities were made to small firms. 

This compares favorably to the 2, 006 calls to develop opportunities and the 1, 806 referrals during the same period a year earlier.

Statistics on awards resulting from subcontracting referrals are not available because experience has demonstrated that it is not possible to obtain satisfactory figures on subcontract awards on a voluntary basis.

Business Opportunity Meetings 

Business Opportunity Meetings were held in Joplin, Missouri; Houston, Texas; Charleston, West Virginia; and 

Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania, during the past 6 months. Because of the favorable response to these meetings, it is anticipated that several others will be held during the last 6 months of the year. 

Business Opportunity Meetings are cosponsored by the 

Small Business Administration, other Federal agencies, and

State and local business groups, and are designed to inform small business owners and managers of opportunities to sell to the Government and to obtain subcontract work. Information on selling to the Government is presented through talks by Government contracting officers, exhibits of actual articles purchased by the various agencies, and displays of bid sets. In addition, detailed counseling to assist individual firms in mapping programs

for selling to the Government is provided by Small Business Administration specialists and by procurement officials of the various purchasing agencies represented at the meeting. 

Exhibits by prime contractors of items for which they need subcontractors are featured at each meeting. Purchasing agents of the prime contractors are present to discuss their needs with small firms and to list qualified firms on their bidders lists for subcontract work or direct purchases. This type of arrangement allows prime contractors to locate many new sources of supply of which they otherwise would be unaware, and enables subcontractors to obtain additional work by meeting with many prime contractors who actually are seeking suppliers. 

Small Business Administration Prime Contract Authority 

The Small Business Administration is empowered to enter into contracts with any Department or Agency of the United States Government to furnish supplies and services and to arrange for the performance of such contracts through subcontracting to small business concerns or others, as may be necessary. 

The Small Business Administration believes that its authority to take prime contracts in order to extend subcontracting should be continued on a stand-by basis for use in emergencies. 

Cases of Procurement Assistance 

The following cases and comments from proprietors of small firms illustrate the wide variety of assistance provided through the Agency's programs: The owner of a small San Diego, California, firm wrote the following to the Agency's Los Angeles Regional Office: "I 

would like to.. express our appreciation for the assistance of your organization, leading to our successful bid for construction of the subject pontoons. The time extension which you requested. enabled our organization to offer these pontoons at a savings of approximately $100, 000 to the United States Government It is very gratifying to know that small businesses can rely on your organization for assistance and counsel. . . . . !! A large corporation had this to say about the work of the Agency's Minneapolis Regional Office in developing subcontracting סוג. et

opportunities for small firms: "We have received a listing of potential subcontractors which you have prepared for us. 

This listing will be of great value in establishing sources for our subcontract work in this area. . We feel that many working relationships will be made between us and small businesses because of your efforts. " A one-man tool and die shop in Grand Island, Nebraska, received a $10,000 Government prime contract through contacts made at the Small Business Opportunity Meeting in Omaha a year ago. The businessman states that he believes opportunity meetings are exactly what the small shop owner needs in order to learn of bidding opportunities in a minimum of time. 

The Agency's San Francisco Regional Office received the following letter from an official of a small firm: "I should like to... thank the Small Business Administration. for the direct assistance and counsel you have so willingly given. . 

You have saved us many hours by directing us to the right agencies and persons to see in a necessarily complex network of Federal Government buying. Small concerns like our own cannot afford a staff to delve into and learn the intricacies of these matters. Therefore, if it were not for your assistance, it is obvious that much of the work for the Government that is now going to small concerns would not be going to them."'

Upon learning that a small firm in Puerto Rico was delinquent on two contracts for the Philadelphia Quartermaster

Depot, the Small Business Administration had its Puerto Rico office contact the firm. The Small Business Administration branch manager there learned that deliveries had been delayed due to circumstances beyond the control of the firm, and that it was desperately attempting to meet its delivery schedule. The Agency passed this information on to the contracting officer and asked that the delivery time be extended without penalty. The contracting officer agreed, and the firm since has made deliveries within the required time. Prompt action by the Agency thus prevented possible termination action or penalty to the firm because of delinquent delivery.

The following letter was received by the Small Business Administration's regional office at Detroit from a small manufacturer who had been awarded a contract for approximately a million and a half dollars: "We take this opportunity to express our

appreciation for the co-operation recently extended by your Agency. 

Your analysis of the financial ability of this company to undertake 

Invitation for Bid was undoubtedly a factor in giving us the opportunity to resubmit a resume of our financing arrangements in order to show our ability to perform. 

Your sound approach to the problems of a small business such as ours is much appreciated by this company.

An official of a large firm wrote this about the Business Opportunity Meetings: "The small business conferences have supplied our company with a means of contacting many concerns which normally would have been bypassed because of the expense involved in otherwise contacting them."

Small Business Administration Liaison Office with Department of 

Defense Another staff member has been added to the Small Busi ness Administration Liaison Office with the Department of Defense. 

Staff members of this office study current defense procurement policies and their effect on small business and recommend changes which would be beneficial to small concerns. The Armed Services 

Procurement Regulations currently are being revised to reflect certain policy changes recommended by this Agency. 

Review of Government Procurement Policies and Procedures 

Small business firms regularly bring to the attention of the Small Business Administration various problems which highlight difficulties experienced by small concerns in selling to the Govern- Agency specialists take up these problems with the various procurement offices on an individual basis. In addition, typical problems are referred to the Task Force for Reviewing of Government Procurement Policies and Procedures for study and recommendation of policy changes to correct these conditions. The task force was established pursuant to Recommendation No. 5 of the first Progress Report of the President's Cabinet Committee on Small 

Business.

The Small Business Administration has assigned a fulltime member to the task force, and another Agency staff member is serving as chairman of the study group that is developing uniform small business set-aside procedures. Other staff members are serving on groups concerned with implementation of the new definition

of small business; with small business participation in Governmentsponsored research and development programs; with simplification of Government marking, packing and packaging specifications; with

Government specifications; and with the drafting of proposed legislation.

Some of the types of difficulties experienced by small firms which have been referred to the task force by the Small 

Business Administration are: 

1. Nonavailability of specifications and drawings. 2. 

Procurement by specifying a brand name or equal. 

Inadequate or inaccurate blueprints. 4. 

Delays in payment. 5. 

Need for contract financing, including progress payments. 6. 

Faulty or highly "critical" inspection. 7. 8. 

Delays in settlement of claims.

Inadequate small business participation in the field of research and development. 

Research and Development Contracts 

According to the Department of Defense, during the first 10 months of fiscal year 1957, small business firms received only 3.9 per cent of the value of research and development contracts awarded to business firms. This percentage must be increased if the over-all scientific knowledge and engineering capacity of the 

Nation are to be utilized. In research and development procurements, the military services seek participation by the best qualified firms, educational institutions, nonprofit organizations, or individuals, in order that the Government will receive the finest products or studies. This policy places a premium on superiority of facilities and experience,

in contrast to supply contracts where the evaluation is limited only to ability to meet specifications.

In research and development, the Small Business Administration is striving for maximum use by the Government of small firms or individuals in those fields which are especially suited to their participation. This would release the larger firms and educational institutions from research and development that could be performed adequately by small business, and enable them to concentrate their abilities on the more complicated fields of basic research and development. 

Small Business Administration staff members counsel small firms and individuals interested in research and development work and guide them to the appropriate procurement officials. In addition, the Agency obtains set-asides on research and development procurements whenever it finds that there will be adequate small business competition for the type of project contemplated. During this past fiscal year, 29 per cent of the value of procurements which were screened by Small Business Administration representatives at the Quartermaster Research and Development Command in Natick,

Massachusetts, were jointly set aside for small business. Progress also is being made in other purchasing offices.

Certificates of Competency 

The Small Business Administration offers what is in effect an appeal procedure for a small firm whose low bid on a Government contract has been rejected on the grounds that the firm lacks the necessary financial and productive capacity. 

Under the Small Business Act, the Small Business Ad ministration is authorized to certify that small firms or small business defense production pools are competent, with respect to financial means and productive capacity, to perform specific 

Government contracts.

Once such a certificate is issued by the Agency, procurement officers are directed under the law to accept it as conclusive, as far as financial and technical requirements of the contract are concerned. 

Certificates of competency are issued under the following conditions: 

1. The Small Business Administration has determined that the firm applying to it for a certificate is the lowest responsive bidder on an advertised purchase or within price negotiation range on a negotiated purchase.

2. 

The procurement officer has questioned the firm's financial ability or productive capacity with respect to the particular contract, and

A survey by the Small Business Adminis tration of the applicant's financial situation and productive facilities indicates that the firm is competent to perform the contract.

When a contract has been awarded as a result of issuance of a certificate, the Small Business Administration maintains a close check on the contractor's progress. If the firm encounters production delays or other difficulties in carrying out the terms of the contract, Agency specialists offer assistance with the problems. 

In the past 6 months there has been an increase in the number of certificate applications involving purchases by civilian Government agencies, such as the Treasury Department, Bureau of Engraving and Printing, and the General Services Administration. 

In the last 6 months, 152 applications for certificates were received, and 63 certificates issued. The table on page 80 shows the number of applications for certificates received, the purchasing agency involved, and the number of certificates issued since start of the Agency.

helpful to them in developing new or improved products, or in entering a field that is new to them.

In carrying out the first two of these functions, the Small Business Administration prepares and issues the Products List Circular, a monthly publication which contains a brief digest of about 100 patented machines, devices or processes. The circular has proved helpful to small manufacturers and to individuals or small concerns interested in finding buyers or producers for items which they, themselves, have patented. The circular is furnished free at present, and about 12, 000 copies are distributed each month. However, plans are being made to place the circular on an annual subscription basis. 

As of June 30, 1957, the Products List Circular had provided information on 2, 172 inventions. Of the 1, 220 inventions abstracted and publicized in the circular during fiscal year 1957, a total of 203 were Government-owned and 1, 017 were privately owned. During this reporting period, the Small Business Administration received 2, 360 inquiries about products and processes listed in the circular. 

Upon request by individual small firms, the Small Business Administration will obtain data on specific methods and processes from Government and industrial sources of information. 

A wealth of technological information of value to small companies is available within the Government. The Products Assistance 

Program is designed to make such knowledge available to small companies. Similarly, small companies frequently need guidance in obtaining technical information and data from commercial sources.

Typical recent requests for information included inquiries. on resilvering mirrors; the feasibility of roasting coffee in sealed cans by the use of high frequency electric current; the manufacture of fiberglass wire screen; manufacture of papier-mache; use of wood charcoal in soil fertilizers; and reproduction of original paintings on ceramic surfaces. 

During the past 6 months, the Small Business Administration answered 5, 181 requests for information on various processes and methods.

Inventory of Small Plant Facilities Small The Small Business Administration's inventory of small plant facilities is an integral part of defense mobilization planning, as a means of assessing the industrial potential and of locating alternate contractor sources. The program is a voluntary one and is operated on a regional basis by the Agency's field offices. concerns can have their facilities listed in the inventory by filling out the appropriate Small Business Administration form or by submitting to the Agency a brochure describing their facilities. The principal information required includes equipment in the plant, number of personnel, products or services furnished, and related data.

At present, the chief uses of the nationwide inventory are to assist in Small Business Administration referral of prime contract opportunities to registered concerns and to enlarge bidders lists at Federal procurement agencies. The inventory also serves as a means of locating small firms with scarce or special purpose machinery required for Government or industrial use. 

As of June 30, 1957, there were 35, 717 small companies registered with the Agency. Most of these were manufacturing plants. 

Loan Appraisals 

During this reporting period, the Small Business Administration broadened and improved its technical loan appraisal activities. 

The Agency's industrial and production specialists now are being used more widely in appraising and evaluating collateral offered as security for loans. To accomplish this purpose, a new appraisal unit has been established within the Agency's Office of Procurement and Technical Assistance. 

In addition to appraising and evaluating collateral, Agency specialists will assist small business firms in connection with loan applications to the Agency, in overcoming technical problems which have led to loans becoming delinquent, and in solving technical problems which, if not resolved, might result in loan delinquencies. 

Technical Production Assistance 

Many small plants lack employees who have the specialized technical experience needed to solve production problems that arise any llo

in the course of the regular work, or when the plants are entering new fields or handling defense contracts which are somewhat different from their normal production. The Small Business Administration helps here through regional office staff specialists who have professional backgrounds in engineering, production, materials, and other areas of the industrial and general production fields. For example, small firms frequently need counsel and aid on the acquisition of new equipment, plant layout, and methods engineering. 

Competition with private engineering and related firms is avoided by Small Business Administration staff members. When an 

Agency production or industrial specialist finds that a small company's problems are of sufficient scope or complexity to require follow-up attention of a professional consultant, he so advises the company. However, many problems which small firms bring to the Agency do not require the services of a professional consultant. 

Materials and Equipment Assistance 

During the last 6 months, there was a better supply of basic materials and production equipment to meet the needs of small business. The steel supply-demand situation, acute during the last 6 months of 1956, eased due to increased supplies resulting from new or enlarged facilities and a reduction in the demands of major consuming industries. The heavy demand for steel plates, structural shapes and tubular products created spot shortages for small firms early in the reporting period, but the shortages eased in recent months.

Among the few shortage situations, strikes at the plants of producers of argon gas created serious production problems for small business, especially nondefense users. In addition, electrolytic nickel continues in short supply. The production of machine tools has, in general, been adequate to meet the needs of small business concerns, although the Agency has been asked frequently to locate commercial sources of used equipment or surplus Government-owned equipment. business:

The Agency continued these functions on behalf of small 1. 

The Office of Defense Mobilization asks the Small Business Administration to review

2. 3. each request from the Department of 

Defense for approval to lease Govern ment-owned production equipment for nondefense use, to see that such leases do not adversely affect the competitive position of small business. 

The Small Business Administration acts as spokesman for small business before and consults with other Government agencies which are considering the adoption or revision of materials and equipment policies, programs, or regulations affecting small business.

Through its regional and branch offices, the Agency provides small firms information on the Government's materials and equipment regulations.

Tax Amortization Assistance 

The Small Business Administration performs two principal functions in the accelerated Tax Amortization Program. At the request of the Office of Defense Mobilization and the delegate agencies, Small Business Administration field offices obtain additional information about small business applications for tax amortization certificates which lack essential data. In other cases, the Agency is asked by small firms to check on the status of their applications and advise them of adverse developments or other circumstances of which they should be informed. A procedure developed last year with the Department of Commerce assures full consideration of small business applications referred by the Office of Defense Mobilization to the Department of 

Commerce for review. Before recommending disapproval of any small business application, the Department asks the Small Business Administration for comment or additional documentation in support of the application.

At the request of the Small Business Administration, the 

Office of Defense Mobilization reactivated the Ad Hoc Committee on Expansion Goals in order to assure adequate representation for small business during discussions of proposed new expansion goals.

As of June 30, 1957, the number of open expansion goals had been reduced to 4, a reduction of 12 during the last 6 months. 

Two of the unfilled goals are for the types of facilities which small concerns are unlikely to construct, but small business enterprises are regularly qualifying under goal number 206, for research and development laboratories for constructing special military equipment, and goal number 224, involving production facilities for military and atomic energy procurement which includes plants, warehouses and other facilities.

This report represents the second consecutive semiannual period during which all of the applications on which small firms solicited the assistance of the Small Business Administration were approved by the Office of Defense Mobilization.

Specifications Assistance 

A continuing problem of small business firms is difficulty in obtaining copies of Government specifications. Many small firms receive bid sets from Government contracting officers without the appropriate specifications, or with the basic specification alone, while important reference specifications are omitted. Frequently, when a small firm writes to the purchasing agency for the needed specifications, heavy demand for them already has exhausted the supply.

The Small Business Administration maintains at its 

Washington and field offices a reference set of the packaging and packing specifications most often required by small concerns in selling their products and services to the Government. 

During this reporting period, the Agency worked closely with the Task Force for Reviewing of Government Procurement Policies and Procedures. Technical guidance was provided to the task force study group which is seeking to simplify Government marking, packing and packaging specifications. In addition, an

Agency staff member is serving on the study group dealing with the over-all area of Government specifications. The Small Business Administration has submitted a number of subjects to the study groups for consideration and possible solution. 11 The Small Business Administration makes available to small firms the "U. S. Government Specifications Directory, a guide to more than 500 places throughout the country where small business owners may refer to Government specifications and indexes. 

The directory discusses the purchasing specifications used by the Government, tells how businessmen may obtain copies of them and provides pointers on using them. 

In the period January 1 through June 30, 1957, the Small

Business Administration received from the General Services Ad ministration 121 proposed new or interim Federal specifications.

These were reviewed by the Agency to make certain their provisions would not be restrictive to small concerns. The Agency's analyses resulted in a number of recommendations to the General Services Administration for deletion of possible restrictive or discriminatory provisions. 

The Small Business Administration also seeks the issuance of new specifications, or revisions in existing ones, to make it possible for small manufacturers to have their products considered in Government purchasing. Toward this end, negotiations were held during the past 6 months with the Quartermaster Corps, Marine Corps, Coast Guard, National Bureau of Standards, and 

General Services Administration. 

Small Business Production Pools 

A small business production pool is an association of small business concerns which have appropriate production facilities and which have:

1. 2. 3. 

Voluntarily joined together to obtain and perform, as a group, contracts for items or services needed in the defense program; 

Agreed upon the form of organization and operating methods of the pool and the relationship of the member firms to the pool; and Obtained Government approval of the pool and its objectives.

Upon request, the Small Business Administration extends advice and assistance in the formation of pools. The Agency's approval of a proposed pool's operations is subject to the concurrence of the Attorney General and the Chairman of the Federal Trade 

Commission.

One new pool was organized and approved during the first half of 1957, the Caledonia County Production Pool Association, consisting of three small firms in Vermont. 

As of June 30, 1957, there were seven active small business production pools. These pools are composed of 54 companies, employing approximately 3, 100 persons. Two of the presently active pools have reported receiving 269 prime contract awards totaling $1,069, 111 and 4 subcontract awards totaling $85, 967. 

Managerial Assistance

During the period covered by this report, the Small Business Administration provided managerial assistance to small firms through publications, cosponsorship of administrative management courses for small business owners and managers, and management counseling.

Management Publications 

As a means of disseminating useful information on sound management practices, the Small Business Administration issues four series of publications: Small Marketers Aids, Management Aids for Small Manufacturers, Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers, and the Small Business Management Series. The first three series are distributed by the Agency without charge;

the

fourth is sold by the Superintendent of Documents at nominal prices. 

A listing of the publications made available during the period from 

January 1 through June 30 is contained in appendix I.

The most recent development in the Small Business Administration's management publication program is the adoption of a classification system for indexing and filing. It does not replace the titling and numbering used previously, but is offered as a supplementary aid to businessmen and others in filing business information.

Small Marketers Aids 

This series of 4-page articles is designed to assist owners and operators of small retail, wholesale and service enterprises. 

The articles, one of which is published each month, deal with management subjects of broad application and are written by specialists in private and public organizations. Thus far, 22 of these Aids have been published. Representative Aids include: "Term Loans in Small 

Business Financing, " "Pricing and Profits in Small Stores," "Buying a Small Going Concern," and "Fundamental Records for Small 

Marketers." To date, 540, 000 copies of these Aids have been distributed, of which 174, 000 copies were distributed during the last 

6 months.

Management Aids for Small Manufacturers 

11 This series is published for use by owners and operators of small manufacturing concerns. Here, also, one new article is published each month. To date, 83 of these 4-page articles have been published. Representative articles include "More for Your 

Storeroom Dollar, " "Reducing the Risks in Product Development, "Utilizing Older Workers in Small Industry," and "Choosing the

Legal Structure for Your Firm. "' The Agency has now distributed 

2,077,000 copies of these Aids, of which 212, 000 copies were distributed during the last 6 months. Because of the continuing interest in the earlier Aids, bound volumes containing the first 63 have been published and are sold by the Superintendent of Documents. Management Aids for Small Business: Annual No. 1, containing the first 31 Aids, is priced at 65 cents. Management Aids for Small Business: Annual 

No. 2, containing Aids 32 through 52, is priced at 55 cents. Manage ment Aids for Small Business: Annual No. 3, containing Aids 53

through 63 is priced at 45 cents. 

More than 41, 500 copies of these three annuals have been sold, 9, 300 having been sold during the last 6 months.

Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers 

These articles also are published on a one-a-month basis and are designed for use by operators of small plants. The 

Aids deal with significant developments in such fields as materials, processes, equipment and maintenance, and are chosen with the help of representative businessmen. To date, 53 of these 4-page articles have been published. Representative recent titles include: "Increased Profits from Industrial Air Conditioning," "Small Busi ness and the Industrial Applications of Atomic Energy," "Control of Expendable Tools," and "Reduce Waste--Increase Profit." The

Agency has distributed 708, 000 copies of these Aids on request, of which 120,000 copies were distributed during the last 6 months. 

As in the case of Management Aids, early articles in the Technical Aids series are bound in permanent form. The first 27 have been published and are sold by the Superintendent of Docu ments as Technical Aids for Small Business: Annual No. 1. This illustrated volume of 229 pages is priced at $1. Total sales to date are over 6, 200 copies, of which 1, 800 copies were distributed during the last 6 months.

Small Business Management Series 

When a management subject cannot be covered adequately in a short leaflet, the Small Business Administration has a leading authority in the field prepare a manuscript on the subject for publication in this series. These publications vary in length from 14 to 

102 pages, and are priced at 15 to 35 cents. Combined sales to date total 512, 278 copies, of which 49, 371 copies were sold during the last 6 months. 

Management Counseling 

The Small Business Administration provides individual counseling on management problems to small business owners. 

Managerial assistance is inherent in the various Small Business Administration activities relating to financial, procurement, production, and products assistance. Problems in these areas involve the application of appropriate managerial techniques. Substantial

research is required to assist with the problems, and for this purpose reference libraries are maintained in the Washington and regional offices. The Agency also works closely with other Federal 

Agencies and Departments, trade associations and universities in obtaining information requested by small business firms. 

Management Courses 

During the first 6 months of 1957, 72 more administrative management courses were cosponsored by the Small Business Administration and educational institutions. This ends the third full school year of the program and brings to 264 the total number of courses cosponsored by the Agency. The courses have been conducted by 100 educational institutions. More than 8, 700 business men and women have attended. Evaluations of the courses by the business owners and operators who attend them continue to reflect almost unanimous approval.

The courses are conducted co-operatively by the Small Business Administration and leading educational institutions for the purpose of improving the management skills of small business proprietors. Typically, a course runs for 8 weeks, 1 evening a week for 2-1/2 hours. Subjects relate to administration planning, organizing, staffing, directing, controlling rather than day-to-day operations. 11 

Continuing its efforts to strengthen and expand this program, the Small Business Administration recently co-operated with the University of Colorado in planning and organizing a second 

Conference on the Problems of Training Small Business Executives. 

Educators located throughout the Nation with experience in conducting administrative management courses for small business owners and managers were invited to participate. The Conference was designed to develop better ways of teaching small business executives to conduct their businesses more efficiently. An outgrowth of this second Conference was the formation of the National Council for 

Small Business Management Development. Committees of the 

Council are working on a permanent organization and are continuing research for the improvement of small business management. third conference is planned for 1958.

Small Business Administration as of June 30, 1957 and prior to audit. It covers only the regular lending operations of the Agency, conducted under the provisions of the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended. The opera- tions with respect to the liquidation of the Reconstruction 

Finance Corporation disaster loans, transferred under the provisions of Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1954, are described in separate exhibits. 2/ Liabilities exclude undisbursed loan authorizations and commitments of $58, 735, 281. 03 as of June 30, 1957, the details of which are shown on the accompanying statement, "Status of Revolving Fund. Footnotes to Statement of Income and Expense - Lending Operations

Business Administration. Excludes $1,850, 229.93 expenses incurred against current year appropriation for other programs provided for in the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended.
